# Request ledger

One line per user request. The desk's own accountability record: it exists
because requests were coming back a second and third time, which always meant
the same thing — the desk routed it, assumed, and never checked.

**CONFIRMED IS NOT PERMANENT.** Later work can break a row that was
genuinely confirmed, and nothing detects that automatically — the auditor
walked it once and moved on. H found this on its own: a crowd regression it had
introduced invalidated an existing CONFIRMED, and it said so and asked for the
re-check rather than leaving the row reading green.

**If your work could plausibly have broken a confirmed row, say so and name
it.** Moving it back to LANDED is the honest thing and it costs one re-walk. A
CONFIRMED row that is quietly false is worse than an OPEN one, because nobody
is looking at it any more.

**EVIDENCE GOES STALE — IF YOUR OWN WORK INVALIDATES IT, UPDATE THE CELL.**
H found the lot-facing row recording *"yaws the world no longer has"*: the
numbers were true when written and a later pass by the same builder moved the
cars. A verifier then chases a world that has gone, and reports a fault that is
only a fault against the old numbers.

This is the same principle as CONFIRMED not being permanent, one step earlier.
Measurements in an evidence cell are a claim about a specific build. If you
move the thing the numbers describe, republish them in the same commit.

**WHEN YOU MOVE A ROW TO LANDED, SAY WHERE TO STAND.** One line: the
station a verifier should judge it from, or the predicate that settles it.

H measured what this is worth. Verifying rows that carried no station, it built
five generic filters and got five wrong sets and **zero real faults**: bench
slats counted as ground, two bunting runs merged into a 109 m gap, a radius test
applied to 4.5 x 1.8 m rectangles, prompts reporting the nearest spot rather
than the tested one, and a building facade matched as "the big sign". Against
that, **ten confirmations, every one from standing somewhere and looking** — and
the two fastest were the two rows whose builder had named the station: I's
*"verify by standing where a person would sit"* and D's `highlightParity`
affordance. One attempt each.

**A row that carries its station is verified in one pass. A row that does not
costs three and may still be abandoned.** The builder knows the station and
writing it costs a sentence; the verifier does not and pays for it five times.

**ONE ROW, ONE WRITER — and builders write only their OWN rows, only
OPEN -> LANDED, only the status and the evidence cell.** Never re-sort, never
reflow, never touch a row that is not yours. Ten agents appending to one file
is a conflict machine: G's church and library work sat unlandable behind a
LEDGER.md conflict where G had moved its pew row to LANDED and E had moved its
fence row in the same three lines. Both edits were correct and the file still
would not merge.

If you find yourself resolving a ledger conflict, take the MOST ADVANCED status
of each row (OPEN < LANDED < CONFIRMED) and keep both sides' evidence — the two
writers are almost always advancing different rows.

**Only the desk or the auditor may write CONFIRMED.** That has not changed.

**Format is parsed. Keep it.**

    | STATUS | owner | request (user's words, trimmed) | evidence |

`STATUS` is one of:

- **OPEN** — routed, not yet landed
- **LANDED** — the builder says it is done, nobody has checked
- **CONFIRMED** — the desk or the auditor saw it working in the world
- **REJECTED** — the user looked and said no; it goes back OPEN with a note

**LANDED is not done.** The only status that counts is CONFIRMED, and only the
desk or the auditor may set it, never the builder that did the work.

`scripts/ledger.sh` lists everything not CONFIRMED. Run it before telling the
user anything is finished.

| CONFIRMED | I | cars on the left row face backwards | H's convention, published so it is not guessed: makeCar builds NOSE-FIRST DOWN -z, so a car at yaw y points its nose at (-sin y, -cos y). Measured in the world: north row (z 8.6) yaw 0.55 -> nose (-0.52, -0.85); south row (z -3.4) yaw 2.59 -> nose (-0.52, +0.85). Both rows therefore face the aisle AND angle WEST, toward the street. If the intent is to angle east, into the lot, both rows are mirrored: use -0.55 and pi+0.55. Geometry cannot tell me which was meant — C's call |  ||  FROM C: already derived per side of the aisle, mirrorYaw(t)=pi-t, so a row added later cannot come out backwards. lot-layout clause 5 reports 18 of 18 nose-out; on :5177, 11 of 11 nose-out, 0 tails. H's remaining question is a DESIGN call, not a bug: both rows angle WEST. That is intended — you drive in from the street heading east, so a westward cant shows the fronts on the way in and lets a car pull straight out toward the exit. **H (verifier): NOT GRADED — the convention has to be pinned before a number means anything.** 14 lot cars, yaws mostly 0 with three at 1.57 and 4.7. The body model puts the FRONT at -z, but which row the user means by "left" and which way it ought to face is their framing, not something I can read off a transform — and guessing it is how this row came back twice. You are quietly left one floor up. **Likely cause, offered because it narrows the search rather than to do C's job:** `jumpTo` sets `SPAWN.gy = 5.4`, but the floor picker carries hysteresis (GOTCHAS §7) and, arriving from above, resolves to the nearest storey at or below where you were — the top landing. **C's own comment anticipated exactly this case** — *"a teleport … can leave lastGy at a height no storey occupies"* — the handler just does not force the floor it asks for. **Controls, so the pass is not vacuous:** a valid floor inside the walk-up (gy 5.4) does **not** trigger a jump, and a lost floor **out on the street** does not either — the `px > 100` gate holds. The mechanism fires when it should and only where it should; it is the landing that is wrong. `scripts/respawn.mjs`, `scripts/respawnband.mjs`. |

The strips are gone and the room got bigger again. **The metric matters more than the number, so here it is explicitly.** *Passage width*: for every free cell, the clear run through it along x and along z, taking the **smaller** — how wide the way is where you stand. I first used *widest clear gap on a line* and it ranked the library **most open of all ten at 12.36 m**, because that measure calls a room open if **one strip of it is** — the exact failure I had warned about, and my instrument could not see it. **A metric that cannot express the fault you are looking for will always clear it.** |

| CONFIRMED | G | hotel exterior looks nice / interior doesnt match the exterior however |  **LANDED (G).** Took ct/vice.ts's own constants inside — RED #8e1f2a, RED_D #5a1520, GOLD_D #8a6a22 — not lookalikes. Patterned carpet for plain tile, hanging glass pendants for flush discs, brass and mirror behind the desk, floor-length drapes. — **AUDITOR CONFIRMED that the interior reads as the hotel's own palette; ONE PART OF THE CLAIM IS NOT ACCURATE, and the reason matters (build 3238fe7a8).** **Delivered:** the lobby reads unmistakably as a red-and-gold hotel — 44.1% of the rendered frame is warm and 42.0% strongly red, against a street exterior that is 0.0% strongly red. Patterned carpet, gold picture rail, brass behind the desk. **The constants are real:** `vice.ts:184` declares `GOLD #d8a83a, GOLD_D #8a6a22, RED #8e1f2a, RED_D #5a1520`. **Two of the three named are used verbatim inside** — `#5a1520` and `#8a6a22` both appear in `int-hotel.ts` as literals. **But `#8e1f2a` — RED itself, the main one — appears in `int-hotel.ts` ONLY IN THE COMMENT that names it.** The wall is `0x6d2029` and the floor `0x5a2430`: close to RED, and **lookalikes are exactly what the row says these are not**. **And none of them is imported.** `vice.ts:184` declares them inside a function scope, so they cannot be, and both files carry duplicate literals. **The values agree today and nothing keeps them agreeing** — change the exterior and the interior silently does not follow. Same shape as the four hand-copied `minFilter` calls H fixed by moving the rule up a level; the fix here is to export the palette from `vice.ts` and import it. Not a fault in what the user sees, and a real fragility in how it is held together. |
| CONFIRMED | F | the interior door doesnt match the exterior doorway | MEASURED BY F: every decidable room mirrors correctly — burger, diner, tax, thrift. The rest have centred doors or publish no frontage and read as UNDECIDABLE rather than as passes. Note F's own correction in F-doorside-tax.md: it briefly reported tax as broken and it is not. AUDITOR to confirm. — **AUDITOR CONFIRMED, reproduced independently (build 974d16648): 4 decidable rooms, 4 mirroring correctly — burger, diner, tax and thrift**, which is F's list exactly. The other six are undecidable and for stated reasons: **church, hotel, library and pawn have centred doors** (0 has no side to mirror), **casino publishes no exterior frontage**, and the **bodega's door is a chamfered corner** — at 45° both normal components are equal, so "which way does the facade run" has no answer. **The interesting part is that I walked into F's own bug before I read F's note about it.** My first version judged *"opposite signs = correct"* with no reference to which way each facade faces — and reported **tax as the single failure**. That is character-for-character the fault F documents in `notes/F-doorside-tax.md`: *"that is only true for buildings on one side of the street"*, and **tax and pawn face −x while thrift and library face +x**. Applying F's actual relation, `worldOffset = side × localOffset` with `side = −normal`, tax passes and the four agree. **F was right to correct itself and right to route rather than reach into `int-tax.ts`; changing that `side: 1` would have broken a room that works.** **Two further bugs were mine alone.** I selected the along-frontage component using the **exterior** normal for both doors, so any room whose interior door faces a different axis had its **half-depth read as an offset** — the church came out at 12.00, which is simply half of its 24 m. And my decidability rule required **both** doors to be centred, which made the library look like a failure when its exterior door is dead centre and therefore cannot decide anything. **Four separate defects between the first run and the last, and every intermediate version produced a confident, plausible table.** The one that mattered was already written down by the builder who hit it first. |

| CONFIRMED | H | this guy is floating | **AUDITOR CONFIRMED (audit/seams, build e2ead9895).** I said last session that my instrument could not see this, because the float is in the FRAME and mesh-bottom-vs-groundAt can never reach it. So I built the one that can: scripts/footpaint.mjs reads each figure's own atlas frame, finds the **lowest OPAQUE pixel**, converts it to a world height, and compares THAT to the ground — it is the painted shoe that has to touch, not the quad. Over 18 citizens (64-row frames): **painted foot vs ground, median 0.000, MAX 0.000, zero figures off the ground.** The proof needs the contrasting number beside it: the QUAD bottom is off the ground on **20 of 20**, and the atlas padding is UNCHANGED at median 0.119 m (C measured 0.108-0.129), still 4 empty rows of 64. H did not repaint the atlas — they dropped the quad by exactly the padding so the painted shoe lands on the ground. **Either number alone fails this row wrongly:** my old quad check would have said 20/20 floating, and a padding check would have said nothing changed. Two figures did read 2.56 m up; they are 2-row-frame objects, not people — part of the park pergola (shots/pf-float.png), up where they belong. Splitting the sample by frame height separates them cleanly: 64-row n=18 off-ground 0, 2-row n=2 off-ground 2. | superseded routing note from 3f5a6b6f1: C measured it world-wide and proved it is the ATLAS, not any call site — seven figures, call-site gap 0.000 for every one, atlas padding 0.108-0.129, and that spread is only each figure's height scale. Cause: the shoe is painted on rows 57-59 of a 64-row frame, so FOUR rows below it are empty, and `geo.translate(0, 0.95, 0)` put the origin at the frame's BOTTOM EDGE rather than at the feet — 4 * 1.9/64 = 0.119 m of transparent padding holding every figure off the ground. Fixed in ONE place: citizens.ts now exports citizenPlane(), which both it and ct/crowd.ts call, because the two copies of those two lines are exactly how the street and the interiors would have ended up 12 cm apart. Measured after: 18 figures world-wide, painted feet to ground min 0.000 max 0.000 — **AUDITOR RE-CONFIRMED after H's crowd regression, build c41170c7a.** H reported that rewriting `citizenPlane` for the seated pose inverted a sign and put the whole crowd 1.66 m underground, which would have made this CONFIRMED false while nobody was looking at it. Re-ran `footpaint.mjs`: **18 citizens, 17 flush with their own ground, 1 exception.** The exception is the **church keeper at −0.18 m**, which is the residual already recorded on the interior-people row (its floor is at 0.18 while the figure sits at 0) — not a new fault and not this row's. **18 is also H's own independent count**, which is worth more than either of us counting alone. **[My instrument was wrong twice and both would have failed this row.]** (1) The headline counted **any mesh sampling a sub-rect of an atlas**, which in this world is stoop steps, hedges and brick as well as people — I photographed the five 'figures' at (−9.2, −13) and they are a **brownstone stoop** (`shots/sunk-side.png`, `shots/sunk-feet.png`), its stepped side cheeks. So '26 of 45 figures off the ground' was a headline over a population that was mostly masonry, while the by-frame-height table underneath it was correct all along. It now headlines the 64-row frames, which are the citizens, and reports the rest as scenery. (2) **`groundAt(x, z) IS NOT A PURE FUNCTION OF x AND z.** In a single page, with the player **never moving** — (198.6, −16.3) at both probes — `groundAt(201.95, −16.5)` returned **5.4 and then 0**, because the player's own ground component went 5.4 → 0 between them. Read cold, that says a citizen is **floating 5.4 m**; walked to, `shots/highfig-up.png` shows it standing on the **301/302 hallway floor** where it belongs. footpaint now re-reads every suspect with the player warped to it, which is the only context in which the answer means anything. Written up for everyone in `notes/groundat-context.md` — every script in `scripts/` that compares a height to `groundAt` at an interior location is reading a context-dependent number. — **AUDITOR: the re-confirmation above was measured on a STALE BUILD; re-done on HEAD (8e78016c9), and the verdict holds with a correction to my instrument.** Port 4184 runs `vite preview`, which serves the pre-built `dist/` and does **not** pick up source changes; it had been up since 22:11 on build `c41170c7a`. I caught it because `__ct.debugSpots` exists at `crosstown.ts:682` and was **not on the running object** while `advanceClock` and `highlightParity`, its neighbours in the same object literal, both were — **two adjacent keys of one literal cannot disagree in a single build.** After `npm run build` and a restart, `debugSpots` appeared. **On the real build: 22 citizen frames, of which 17 STANDING — and 0 of 17 more than 3 cm off the ground.** The other five are **SEATED**, and that distinction is the correction: four casino gamblers read 0.165 m "off the floor" and each sits at **exactly 0.00 m** from a registered `"sit at the slot"` seat of pose height **0.64** — a bar stool. **Feet that hang above the floor are what sitting on a stool looks like.** "The painted shoe must touch the ground" is a claim about standing people, and checking a seated one against the floor is the same category error as checking a quad bottom instead of a painted foot — the exact mistake this row exists to record. `footpaint.mjs` now splits seated from standing off the seat registry. **The fifth seated figure is the church keeper** at (676.6, −5.65), on a **0.54 m** pew, painted foot 0 against a floor at 0.18 — so the −0.18 residual noted on the interior-people row is real and is about its PLACEMENT (figure at absolute 0, floor at 0.18), not about sitting. **[The disclosure]** three of my confirmations were made after 22:11 and therefore on `c41170c7a`: this one, the park bench-backs row, and the three room-301 window rows. **I re-ran all three on HEAD** — bench backs returned byte-identical numbers (0 of 9, 23.4/5.9 down to 6.15/5.15) and the window well returned identical geometry; only this row moved. **A stale build is not detectable from the measurements it produces** — every number I took was internally consistent — which is why the build stamp has to be read rather than the results trusted. Built as a real light well. — **AUDITOR CONFIRMED on the geometry, with one limit stated (build cba9c99e5).** Measured the meshes outside room 301's window wall: the well spans **x 201.20 → 202.40 = 1.20 m deep**, which is the halving from 2.4 the user asked for, **2.18 m across**, with **both side returns present as separate faces** (z −11.60 and z −9.42), a far wall plane at x 202.39, and a **floor at y −2.50 — 4.1 m below eye height**. **That is the claim that mattered: it is real geometry, not a painting.** A painted backdrop cannot have two returns at different depths and a floor four metres down; those are four distinct meshes at four distinct distances. The depth, the returns and the drop all check out, and the far window is gone. **[The limit]** I did not manage to photograph the view *through* the window. Room 301 spawns at (198.6, −16.3) and the well sits at z ≈ −10.5, so the window is elsewhere in the room; four warps put me in the corridor or inside a wall. **The geometry is verified and the framed view is not** — if the desk wants that shot, the window opening needs locating first. — **AUDITOR: THE LIMIT IS NOW CLOSED — I have photographed the view THROUGH the window (build c41170c7a), and in doing so I have to correct my own numbers above.** **The window is in the WEST wall**, not the east: opening at **x 196.73…196.87, z −16.90…−15.60, y 6.25…7.55** — 1.3 m square, sill 0.85 m above a floor at 5.40. **The shaft outside it, measured:** far wall at **x 195.60**, so **1.13 m deep** — the halving from 2.4 the user asked for; side returns at **z −15.30 and −17.20**, so **1.90 m across**; **floor at y 1.15, which is 4.25 m below the room floor**; and a **drainpipe at x 195.68…195.80 running the full height y 1.15 → 12.0 with three brackets** at y 3.10, 5.90 and 8.70. That is C's own description — *"1.9 m across x 1.2 m deep of sooted brick with both side returns and a floor three storeys down in the dark, plus a drainpipe"* — matched item for item. **SEEN, which is the half I could not deliver before:** `shots/r301-W.png` is the whole window from where you stand in the room — frame, mullions, sill with a cactus and a mug, sooted brick beyond. `shots/well301-down.png` looks down through the glass and **both side returns converge in perspective with the drainpipe at its own depth and the shaft falling away into darkness** — a painted backdrop cannot produce converging returns and a pipe at a different distance. `shots/well301-right.png` shows the left return receding **at eye level, standing**, so the depth is not something you only get by leaning out. **[Correcting myself]** my earlier evidence on this row measured **a different shaft** — x 201.20→202.40, returns at z −11.60 and −9.42, floor at y −2.50 — which lies **east** of the room, while this window faces **west**. The verdict it supported was right and the numbers under it belonged to another light well. **It also explains the limit I filed:** I derived my four camera stations from that shaft's coordinates, which is precisely why all four put me in a corridor or inside a wall. I recorded that as "the window opening needs locating" when the truth was that I had already located the wrong one. **A measurement of the wrong object supports a correct verdict just as comfortably as a measurement of the right one** — the well I measured happened to be built to the same 1.2 m depth, so nothing in the numbers looked odd. |

The **0.95 is the opening and the 0.97 the leaf** — so the leaf is **wider than the hole it closes**, which is the whole fix: it shuts onto the wall face and overlaps the jambs instead of sitting inside the reveal, and the see-through strip at both jambs cannot exist. (C states 0.99; I measure 0.97, and a door's world bbox narrows when it is ajar, which it is at spawn.) **The part worth keeping is how the third gap was found:** C measured **all four edges** rather than trusting two head-on screenshots, and turned up 0.025 m of lit hall over the head — *"invisible at eye height"*. **A gap you cannot see from where you stand is still a gap**, and only enumerating the edges finds it. That is the same lesson as my own gutter-joint and gap-band misses. |

The auditor's own 240 s numbers below do not depend on it and stand. **CORRECTION, added on a later pass: the before-figure quoted next has been RETRACTED BY H.** `notes/H-crossing-pileup.md` establishes that the old `jam` counter incremented on any frame with somebody inside the 0.7 m lookahead — *including a healthy follow at matched pace* — so 29.8 s measured PROXIMITY, not standing still. **This confirmation still stands on my own after-measurements, which are unaffected and have since been repeated on the world the user plays; but the comparison below was against a baseline that does not mean what it says, and I should not have leaned on a builder's before-number without asking how it was counted.** Originally written as: Against H's (withdrawn) before-figure of *one walker stationary 29.8 s of a 60 s minute*: **worst stationary run now 16.0 s**, and it is a `window` act mid-block at (−6, −25.6), **not a crossing endpoint**. All six walkers moved 87–250 m; none was ever motionless for the sample. **Largest pile-up seen anywhere: 2 walkers within 1.5 m** — there is no queue. **POSITIVE CONTROL, and it caught my own blind spot:** my first two runs reported "no jam at the crossing" over 90 s in which **nobody crossed at all** — 0 of 2172 samples in the roadway — which is an empty set, not a pass. The crossings are both at the junction (`crowd-net.ts:152`), so with 6 walkers spread over ~110 m of block a short sample never reaches them. Extending to 240 s produced **3 kerb-to-kerb crossings and 222 samples of walker-in-roadway time**, with two walkers standing at (11.7, −96.7) and (11.58, −97) — the `n-bodega` / `n-corner` endpoints H identifies as the cause. So the crossing was exercised and did not jam. **One residual, flagged not buried:** a single uninterrupted `window` act measured 16–18 s across four runs against `WAIT.window = [5, 12]` in crowd.ts:257. My still-threshold is 0.08 m/s, so ~2 s of easing in and out at each end may account for it and I cannot separate the two with this instrument — worth a glance from H, and not a jam. scripts/crowdjam.mjs. **H ANSWERED IT: not a fault, and not the instrument's threshold either — it is the TIMESTEP CLAMP.** `src/main.ts:107` does `dt = Math.min(clock.getDelta(), 0.05)`, so below 20 fps sim time and wall time are different clocks. Measured headless with a probe reading walkers once a frame: **13.2 fps, mean frame 75.9 ms, 263 of 264 frames over the 50 ms clamp, sim time advancing at 0.659x wall.** A 12 s errand is therefore 18.2 s of wall clock, and the observed 18.6 s matches within the sampling interval. Traced it by splitting each stationary run into before/during/after the errand: **all of it was during** (before 0.0, after 0.2, jam 0.0, ghost 0.0) with `wait` never rising (0 rises in 12 runs), so it was one errand draining slowly, not two chained. `WAIT.window = [5,12]` is correct and crowd.ts is unchanged. Written up as **GOTCHAS 37** because it will bite any duration measured from outside the world. Separately worth someone's attention and NOT builder-owned: a player below 20 fps sees the world in slow motion, not merely at fewer frames. | superseded builder note: Root cause is NOT the crossing's width, it is its ENDS: both crossings begin and end on nodes where people deliberately stand still (`n-bodega` is a `door`, 4-8 s; `n-corner`/`w-corner` are `corner` acts, 1.5-4 s). Measured: walkers parked ON a crossing endpoint for 110 samples of one minute, 74 on n-bodega. A parked walker then read as a HEAD-ON MEETING (`theirs <= 1e-4`), so the arriver gave way for the whole errand and whoever came up behind them did too. And the existing "blocked too long, re-plan" escape sat inside `if (!placed)`, which a held walker never reaches — it sets step=0, so its own current position places fine. **[WITHDRAWN by H: this measured proximity, not stalling — see the correction at the head of this row.]** Fixed at the walker: a parked walker is an obstacle to go ROUND, not someone to yield to; jam counts frames that got nowhere rather than frames with anyone ahead; escalation 1.0 s yield -> step round -> 2.0 s reroute; `net.route(from,to,avoid)` strikes the blocked node out and re-routes the same destination round it; crossings given 1.3 m of lateral width vs a walk's 0.55 so people cross abreast in lanes. AFTER, 60 s: worst jam **29.8 s -> 0.0 s for all six**, 0 of 3090 samples off the walk. **NOT PROVED: I never reproduced a visible pile-up** — max 2 walkers at the junction, and street transits were 0 both before and after, so the throughput numbers are not evidence about the crossing. notes/H-crossing-pileup.md | |

(3) THE OLD BUG KEPT OUT: each shoe sits under ITS OWN leg, ankle on that leg's centre, rather than at a separately capped offset — which is what stops the "two shoes floating beside the person" regression at long stride. VERIFIED THREE WAYS: `scripts/feet-check.mjs` — **16 profile cases, 8 unmirrored and 8 mirrored, every one points its toe the way it walks, both atlas columns covered**; the code, for the stride-0 leg split; and `shots/citizen-range.png` looked at directly — 12 people, profile column, toe forward with the heel stub behind and two legs visible at rest. That is exactly the right shape — a front or three-quarter view cannot express a toe direction, and the side view is the one the user complained about. H's diagnosis is the sharpest thing in this row: *"a foot symmetric about the ankle cannot say which way it points, and the eye resolves that as backwards"* — the user's own word, explained rather than paraphrased. **Walked** (`shots/ft-crop2.png`, a citizen moving at 1.11 m/s shot from perpendicular, 3.2 m off): **two legs** on a walking figure, the **shoe extending forward of its leg**, and the walker travelling screen-right with the **toe pointing screen-right** — it points the way they walk. The shoe sits under its own leg, not floating beside it, so the long-stride regression H guarded against is not present either. All three sub-faults visible as fixed. **[My own instrument, corrected]** my first atlas read grabbed a **13×12 prop sprite** and reported all four of its columns "symmetric" — a confident answer about the wrong object. Enumerating every atlas-framed billboard and taking the tallest frame found the citizen sheet. *Taking the first match of a broad filter is how you measure something that was never the subject.* |
| CONFIRMED | H | "im literally stuck here" — parked cars leaving a gap the player fits into but cannot leave | **Already constrained; verified rather than assumed.** `crosstown.ts` draws each parked car from the seeded distribution and then runs `nudgeClear` (ct/gap.ts) with a 4.5 m reach, taking the NEAREST legal spot — so the spread the distribution chose survives and only the trap is removed, which is what "constrain the draw, do not go back to hand-placing" asks for. MEASURED across all 411 colliders in the world: 215 pairs sit within 1.6 m of each other, 64 of those gaps fall in the player's 0.40-0.95 m trap band — **but none of the 64 involves a kerbside parked car.** They are the idle vehicle pool (parked far off the map at x~435 and x~1074 by design, `traffic.ts:136`) and other modules' props. Of the pairs involving parked cars: **0 in the trap band.** **And none of the 64 traps a CITIZEN either**, which was the open question from the frozen-in-the-road item: a citizen is 2*CIT_R = 0.56 m wide, so its danger band (0.56-0.95) sits INSIDE the player's (0.40-0.95) and the existing constraint already covers it. A gap under 0.56 m cannot be entered at all. So "none on the block" is not what I measure. **It does not matter, and here is why.** I re-ran the whole-world flood as a **disc of radius 0.36** — the player's own footprint — and the walkable world is **8593 m² in ONE connected region**. Three pockets exist and all three were already established as unreachable behind continuous railings (two churchyard side strips and a park bed); **none involves a car**. **Not one of the 55 on-block gaps isolates anything.** **The methodological point:** a gap NARROWER than 0.72 m cannot be entered by the player at all, and a gap WIDER than 0.72 m that can be entered can also be left the same way — unless what lies beyond it is closed. **So a band-gap census cannot decide trapping; only connectivity can.** H's constraint on the parked-car draw is sound and their parked-car figure holds (0 parked-car pairs in the band); the wider count is simply measuring a quantity that does not determine the fault. Both of us arrive at "nobody is trapped", and the flood is the part that proves it. scripts/trapgaps.mjs, stucksweep.mjs. |
| CONFIRMED | H | "textures on back of truck are janky" — tailgate aliasing into a checkerboard | **Two faults, and the second is a trap.** (1) MIPMAPS ON A VEHICLE: `pixTex` returns NearestMipmapNearest, right for a wall and wrong for a car — at the grazing angle you see a parked truck from, the mipmap drops to a lower level and the dither in it crawls into a checkerboard. GOTCHAS 4 already had the answer, and the bed skin, tailgate, bed floor and bus roll sign each set `minFilter = NearestFilter` BY HAND. Four copies of one rule is the tell it belonged a level up, so it now lives in `flatT`, which every textured vehicle material is built through. Verified **69 car textures + 14 bus textures, ZERO still mipmapped** (bus located by its 9.1 m length; my first cut matched on texture size and swept up every facade and ground texture in the world, which are not mine). (2) THE ASYMMETRIC TAIL LIGHTS WERE NOT THE LIGHTS. Mirroring each rear face against itself: the slab's rear face was already perfect, and the tailgate had exactly THREE bad texels — x=24 vs mirror x=33, body colour one side, latch colour the other. It was the LATCH, and the cause is PARITY: 0.16 of a 58-wide gate is 9 texels, and a 9-wide feature cannot be centred on an even canvas — it always lands half a texel off and re-centring does not help. Width now rounded to even. AFTER: **rear slab 464 mirror pairs, 0 structural differences; tailgate 580 pairs, 0 structural differences.** The lights were symmetric by construction all along, and the earlier asymmetric-lights report on the bed was a z-fight — this is a third separate thing in the same place. | — **AUDITOR CONFIRMED (build 72e469fd6).** The measurable claim verifies exactly: **zero vehicle textures are mipmapped.** Every texture reached through a vehicle group carries `minFilter = NearestFilter` and `generateMipmaps = false` — 201 car textures and 7 bus textures by my count (H says 69 and 14; I dedupe across every vehicle INSTANCE rather than per kind, so the totals differ and the property does not). **Seen at the angle the fault lived at:** `shots/tg-graze.png`, the tailgate viewed from ~7.5 m at a grazing angle, which is where a mipmap drop crawls into a checkerboard. The face is clean — solid body colour, silver bumper strip, two tail lights, a small latch detail, **no checkerboard and no crawling dither**. **Worth keeping from H's note:** four separate places each set `minFilter` by hand, and *"four copies of one rule is the tell it belonged a level up"* — it now lives in `flatT`, which every textured vehicle material is built through. That is the difference between fixing four symptoms and fixing the cause. |

| CONFIRMED | H | the doors are misaligned. i think the worker doesnt realize they need to confirm the logic independently per side of the car | **AUDITOR CONFIRMED (audit/seams, build 103766f18) — and I checked it the way the user asked, independently per side, because checking one side is how this survived.** The body is a single BoxGeometry with a 6-slot material array, which is exactly the shape of the original fault, so I worked at that level: for each material GROUP I derived `u → z` from **that group's own vertices** rather than assuming a convention, read **that group's own map**, and converted its dark columns to world z. **(1) MIRROR — fixed.** `material[0].map === material[1].map` is **false** on all four kinds; each flank carries its own paint. **(2) ALIGNMENT — fixed.** Sedan shuts read **−1.008 / 0.164 / 1.055** on +x and **−0.961 / 0.211 / 1.102** on −x, bracketing H's −0.98 / 0.19 / 1.08; the rear shut is at ~1.05–1.10, not the old 1.40, so it now sits under the pillar. **Both flanks agree on all four kinds:** sedan 9 features, pickup 7, hatch 10, van 7. **[My own threshold was wrong and I nearly filed a false fault]** my first pass reported "hatch: SIDES DISAGREE, 8 of 10". The per-feature differences are *perfectly uniform* — sedan **all 47 mm**, van **all 48**, pickup **all 31–32**, hatch **79** with two at half — and a real misalignment varies per feature while a constant offset is a sampling artifact: I read each column at its centre `(x+0.5)/W`, and the two faces' UVs run in opposite world directions, so the same painted edge is reported one column apart. | CONFIRMED | H | "this red guy glitches back and forth as he walks sometimes idk why" | **Already fixed by earlier work, and I proved it rather than asserting it.** The desk listed three candidates; all three are in `crowd.ts` now — sticky lateral offsets with a pair-parity tie-break (avoidance oscillation), heading EASED toward the velocity at dt*7 instead of taken raw (the per-frame velocity carries every lateral correction), view hysteresis of 0.7 sectors, and depenetration with a last-known-good fallback. MEASURED, 2 min, 6 walkers, sampling every frame: **atlas view A->B->A flip-flops = 0** for all six. Flips do occur (0.5-9.5/min) but every one is ONE-WAY — a walker genuinely turning, or the view angle to the player genuinely sweeping as they pass close, which is correct. **POSITIVE CONTROL (GOTCHAS 34), because a zero is worthless otherwise:** dropping the hysteresis from 0.7 to 0.0 brings the fault straight back — bounces on 5 of the 6 walkers, up to 2/min, with A->B->A returns in as little as 47 ms. So the detector fires when the fault is present, and the hysteresis is load-bearing. Physical oscillation checked separately: **3 direction reversals in 2 min**, every one dot = -1.00 (an exact 180°) at x = ±6.0 on the walk centre line at 0.5-1.28 m/s — that is the deliberate doubling-back (`c.back = rnd() < 0.22`), not a twitch. **What I could NOT reproduce:** the user's exact geometry, two citizens in the narrow lane between the shopfront and the parked truck. The lane does admit two abreast — offsets span 2*STRAY = 1.10 m and `clearOfPeople` needs 0.46 m of separation — so it is not a layout fault, but if the user still sees it there, that geometry is where to look. Also note every number here was taken at 13 fps headless (GOTCHAS 37). | — **AUDITOR CONFIRMED (build 170a5afa6).** "Back and forth" is a direction reversal, so I measured exactly that: consecutive movement vectors more than 90° apart while actually moving, 6 walkers, 219 s at 8 Hz, ~8,000 moving samples. **One reversal in total, with a longest run of consecutive flips of 1.** An isolated reversal is an about-face at the end of a route; oscillation would show repeated runs of 2 or more. There is none. **[My own summary line was wrong and I am not hiding it]** the script printed *"1 reversals — back-and-forth is present"*, because I had it fire on any count above zero. The per-walker table underneath it shows the truth. **Third time this audit a one-line verdict has contradicted the data directly above it** (benchlean's tiltX, the hatch's 60 mm tolerance, now this) — when they disagree, the table is right and the verdict is a bug. |
| CONFIRMED | H | "these people are stuck" — pedestrians frozen IN THE ROAD | **They had overshot a crossing, and the bug was mine.** Arrival was `hypot(B - position) < 0.45`, which only works while a walker stays near its edge's centre line — and I gave crossings 1.3 m of lateral offset last session so people could cross abreast. A walker in a wide lane is never within 0.45 m of its target node, so it never arrives: it walks past B along the edge's direction for ever, into the carriageway and off the block, until a collider halts it. They are not wedged, they have overshot and been stopped by the first thing they hit. Found by DIRECTION, not guesswork: escapees travelled (-0.21,-0.96); the side crossing n-bodega->s-win1 runs (-0.26,-0.96). Fixed by measuring arrival as a projection ALONG the edge (independent of lateral offset), plus clamping the perpendicular offset back to a walk's width on arrival so nobody stops for an errand 1.3 m into the road. Depenetration also now PREFERS THE PAVEMENT — the shortest way out of a kerbside car is usually roadward, so all four exits are scored (push distance + 1.4x distance from the line you were walking) rather than just measured. Effect not separable from the arrival fix at this sample size; not claiming a number for it. MEASURED, 24 walkers / 4 min / legality taken from the crossings' own segments and allowance: **illegal carriageway samples 242 -> 19 of ~20000 (1.24% -> 0.09%), longest illegal run 71.4 s -> 5.1 s**, and that 5.1 s walker is MOVING at 1.28 m/s 1.5 m off a crossing centre line — inside its lane. Crossing use rose over the same window (979 -> 2129 samples), so this is not fewer-people-in-the-road-because-fewer-people-cross. | — **AUDITOR CONFIRMED (build 170a5afa6), and it corrects a blind spot in my own crossing row.** Watched 6 walkers for 241 s at 4 Hz, tracking time spent inside the carriageway and whether they were MOVING while there. **Worst stationary spell in the road: 0.0 s** — nobody was frozen for as long as 3 s. Only two walkers entered the road at all (9.8 s and 20.5 s in total, longest unbroken spell 10.3 s, which is what crossing takes), and **0 samples had a walker off the block entirely**, so the overshoot-to-infinity H describes is gone. **The correction:** my earlier crossing confirmation cited *"219 of 8652 samples with a walker in the roadway"* as a positive control proving crossings were happening. **Roadway presence does not distinguish a walker crossing from a walker stranded** — and stranded-in-the-road is precisely the fault in this row. The conclusion was right, but I had not measured what I was claiming; now I have, and the roadway time is all moving. |

| CONFIRMED | E | side benches have backs which are backwards? | LANDED, and it was the TENTH orientation bug. This world has two yaw conventions differing by a z-flip: a mesh at `rotation.y = t` faces `(sin t, cos t)`, the camera at yaw `t` looks along `(sin t, −cos t)`. `facingIn` gave the mesh value to `ctx.seat`, so the bench faced the park and the SITTER faced the wall. `E-benchface` shared the mistake and returned 9/9 twice. Fixed, positive-controlled (old yaw → "4 of 9 face out"), and verified by SITTING in each. `E-seatreach` 9/9 reachable — one bench could not be sat on at all. — E — **AUDITOR CONFIRMED — tested by what a SITTER SEES, which is what the row is about (build c41170c7a).** For each of the nine park seats I cast along the seat's own facing and measured how far before something blocks it, then did the same behind. **All nine have more open ground in front than behind** — 23.4 vs 5.9, 23.2 vs 5.2, 22.2 vs 4.2, 21.9 vs 3.7, 21.9 vs 5.4, 13.4 vs 4.4, 25 vs 14.2, 25 vs 2.7, and the tightest 6.2 vs 5.2. **0 of 9 face the closer side**, against E's positive control of "4 of 9 face out" on the old yaw. **This also settles an ambiguity in my own earlier attempt.** I had measured the dot product to the park CENTRE and flagged the bench at (−21.5, −84.2) as facing away. It has **25 m in front and 14.2 m behind** — it faces the open park, just not toward the middle. **"Faces the centre" was never the right test for a bench; "has the open side in front of it" is.** **[Instrument note]** my first run of this returned 0.4 m in BOTH directions for eight of nine seats, because the seat pose sits inside the bench's own collider and the ray hit the furniture immediately. Excluding the box the sitter is already in turned a meaningless symmetric answer into a clear one — and a symmetric answer is exactly what a broken ray looks like. |

Rejecting again, for a different and worse reason. **[Near-miss recorded]** on the way here I measured every interior keeper as "SUNK 1.7–1.8 m below its floor" and nearly filed a world-wide fault. **That is the quad, not the figure**, and it is the exact trap I identified and solved in the floating-citizen row — I had the right instrument sitting in `scripts/` and reached for a bounding box instead. The 1.903 m painted-foot gap is the real number; the 1.73 m quad offset is not. **Wider concern for the desk:** if the keepers are displaced world-wide this also bears on the CONFIRMED row *"interior people on the 8-angle atlas"*, which I confirmed when the tax preparer was plainly visible behind his desk. Worth one look across the ten rooms. — **AUDITOR CONFIRMED (build 9d020db2a), and this overturns my own first rejection, which had the room the wrong way round.** **Which side is public, established from the world rather than assumed:** `roomDims` puts the library door at **local z +11 with an inward normal (0, −1)**, so **customers enter at the +z end and walk toward −z**. The counter spans **z 4.84…5.56**; the librarian stands at **z 4.20**, on the **−z side, 0.64 m clear of its near edge** — the **staff** side. **Seen from both sides, which is what makes it evidence:** from the **customer** side at (916.5, 7.0), `shots/libd-customer-at.png` — the counter fills the foreground and she is **cut off at the chest by it**, facing out. From the **staff** side at (916.5, 1.5), `shots/libd-staff-side.png` — she stands **clear and full-length**, head to feet, with the entrance door behind her. A figure that is occluded from one side and whole from the other is behind the thing that occludes it. **[Correcting myself]** my first rejection measured her at z 4.45 against a counter at 4.84…5.56 — **the same geometry that is there now** — and called it *"0.39 m in front of its near edge, the customer side"*, on the stated ground that *"the room she serves is toward −z (the entrance end)"*. **The entrance is at +z.** I then photographed her from z 1.4, 2.6 and −1.5 — **all three behind the counter** — saw her whole body, and read that as proof she stood in front of it. She was behind it the whole time and I was standing beside her. **My second rejection was right** — she genuinely was not rendered, the world-wide keeper regression — but the first was a frame error of exactly the kind the cat row records: *an offset is only right in the frame it was computed for, and stating your frame is necessary and not sufficient — it has to be the user's frame.* Here the user's frame is a reader walking in at the door. |

The fix that caught it was cheap: count DISTINCT LEVELS in a column rather than look at one line. Two levels means a step; twenty-one means a stair. | CONFIRMED | F | make sure all the clocks throughout the world (library, diner, etc. te | **LANDED (F).** `room.clock()` kit primitive so every clock reads game time from one source. 13:30 -> hour hand -0.790 (want -0.785, halfway between 1 and 2, so it CREEPS); 16:00 -> -2.099 (want -2.094). Diner and library return identical angles. Unconverted clocks in other owners' files listed for routing. — **AUDITOR CONFIRMED, and found by BEHAVIOUR rather than by looks (build 06f0a1eca).** I did not go looking for things shaped like clocks; I set game time and asked what followed it. Snapshot every thin mesh's orientation at **13:00**, again at 13:00 a moment later as a **control** for things that move on their own (citizens, traffic, bunting — 2 meshes, dropped), then at **16:00**. What turned with the hour is a clock hand by definition. **Two clocks in the world follow game time — the DINER at (754.6, 0.3) and the LIBRARY at (920, −10.9) — and they are not merely consistent, they are IN PHASE.** Absolute hand angle **−0.529 at 13:00 and −2.100 at 16:00 on both, identical to 0.0000 rad**. Each turned **1.571 rad over three hours = 30°/hour**, which is right for a 12-hour dial, and the absolute angles are the correct ones (13:00 wants ±0.524, 16:00 wants ±2.094). **F reports −2.099 for 16:00; I read −2.100 without having seen that number when I measured.** **Why phase and not just rate.** Two clocks can each advance exactly 90° over three hours and still be four hours apart — *"they moved the same amount"* is not *"they tell the same time"*, and my first pass only compared the deltas. It reported agreement, and it would have reported agreement for two clocks four hours out. **[Scope, stated because the user's ask is broader than this row.]** The ask is *all* the clocks. **Exactly two follow game time**, which is what F converted; F says so plainly and lists the rest for routing, and G's hotel row describes a lobby clock **deliberately stopped at ten to five** — a period detail, not a fault, and the one exception that should stay. **I could not enumerate the unconverted ones.** Searching for clock-*faces* by shape returned **223** candidates, then **56** when restricted to round geometry, and they are meter boxes, sign panels and ceiling lamps. **A list that size is a broken filter, not a finding**, so I am not publishing a count. Behaviour identifies a clock; shape does not. — **2nd VERIFIER (A): F's CLAIM VERIFIED EXACTLY, but the USER'S ASK IS NOT MET YET — one clock is still wrong, so I am not marking this CONFIRMED.** Not a standing station: two clocks in two rooms cannot be seen at once, which is why the user had to ask at all, so the reading is hand angles. Hands found structurally — `interior.ts` builds them as PlaneGeometry of fixed WIDTH (0.020 hour, 0.013 minute) with length scaling off the dial, so width identifies a clock at any size. **Measured: 13:30 → hour −0.7863 (ideal −0.7854, so it creeps as F says); 16:00 → −2.0953 (ideal −2.0944). DINER (754.6, 2.15, 0.3) and LIBRARY (920, 2.6, −10.9) return byte-identical angles at both times, and the hands moved between them.** All five of my assertions pass. **BUT ONLY TWO CLOCKS USE THE KIT**, and confirming on a population of two when the user wrote "all the clocks throughout the world" is the narrow-population error. **THE TAX OFFICE CLOCK IS A PAINTED TEXTURE AND READS ~4:50 FOREVER:** `int-tax.ts:298` draws the hands into a 32×32 canvas at fixed angles — hour 2.1 rad = 4.01 o'clock, minute −1.1 rad = 49.5 min — and it hangs at (1080, 2.18, −4.2) with rotation 0 that never changes. The helper does what the row says it does. **(1) THE STATED DRIFT RANGE IS WRONG.** The row says *"worst channel drift 1-4 across three real cases"*; its own script prints **6, 3, 4** at HEAD, so the worst is **6**, not 4. Still small — 6/255 will not visibly move anyone's artwork — but an adopter deciding whether `slabTex` is safe for approved artwork reads that range as measured, and it no longer is. Worth one edit, not a re-run. **(2) THE STATION NEEDS A CAVEAT: the proof CANNOT RUN against a preview.** On the built bundle it exits **3** with `ABORT: slabTex not importable from the bundle`. Exit 3 is the correct code (§32, nothing measured) but the row does not say a **dev** server is required, so a verifier who does the obvious thing gets an abort and may read the helper as broken. It is the §28 trap — dev and the bundle resolve differently — and it is the same one that cost me a round on `interiors-walk.mjs`. Add `npx vite` to the check-from. **Scope, from my side of the class:** of the **61** up-facing untextured surfaces on the block, only **25 are surfaces you can stand on** (`scripts/D-paving-vs-trim.mjs`); the other 36 are mouldings, rail caps and a dumpster floor. F's account — *"the heading was ALREADY square, the POSITION was wrong"* — is what the world shows. **[One honest qualification and one correction of mine.]** The door sits in a **chamfered** corner, so "perpendicular to the wall door" has two readings: square to the room's axes (yaw 0, what you get) or square to the 45° chamfer itself (yaw −0.785). I am confirming the first, because it is the one that puts the shop in front of you rather than a diagonal into a corner. **And my first run reported the arrival 135° out** — my yaw helper was `atan2(−nx, nz)`, which points the opposite way down the normal; the camera looks along `(sin y, −cos y)`, so it must be `atan2(nx, −nz)`. The real offset from the chamfer normal is 45°, not 135°. **The yaw convention has now cost this project eleven orientation bugs and one of them is mine.** The helper is checked against a known case at run time — `(0,−1)` must give yaw 0 — so it cannot be wrong silently again. |

| OPEN | DESK | the player spawns in 301 and cannot use ANYTHING in it — the [E] sight ray is built from a constant eye | **FOUND BY A while verifying C's "close this door" row; it is NOT C's door.** `crosstown.ts` builds the visibility ray from `new THREE.Vector3(px, 1.6, pz)`, but `rig.pos.y` is height ABOVE THE CURRENT FLOOR (a constant 1.62 on the street and in a third-floor room alike) and the floor is `apt.gy()`. True eye = gy + rig.pos.y. **Street 0.14 → 1.76 (error 0.16); every ground-floor interior → 1.62 (0.02); APARTMENT 301 floor 5.40 → 7.02, error 5.42 m.** So in 301 the ray starts 5.4 m BELOW the floor the player stands on, aims at groundPick+1.1 = 6.5, travels up through the floor slab and is stopped by it — `canSee` false for everything. 301 is the only non-zero floor in the world and it is the room the player STARTS in. **What a player gets:** one step from spawn, standing **0.23 m** from "sleep until morning" (reach 1.35), twelve facings, nothing offered ever. **NOT the arrival latch** — three probes stalled at 1.15/0.66/1.16 m, a few cm short of the 1.2 m clear, because 301 is small and walking is relative to facing; turning first and trying all eight directions clears it at **1.48 m**, and the spot is still dead on the walk back. **This blocks C's row** and explains E's four dead presses. `notes/A-spawn-room-is-dead.md`; `scripts/A-eye-height-holds.mjs` registered in checks.mjs and RED ON PURPOSE — it asserts the symptom, not the eye arithmetic, so it goes green when the fix lands. crosstown.ts is DESK-owned (OWNERSHIP.md:95) and I have not touched it. — A |
